\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}_{T_{k}}(t_{k})

Figure 1:
Finite temporal resolution in a quantum process:
between interventions, each Hamiltonian evolution is time-averaged over the waiting times between interventions
t_{0},\,t_{1},\cdots,\,t_{k}
with corresponding average waiting times
\tau_{0},\,\tau_{1},\cdots,\,\tau_{k}
, over a probability distribution with
PDF
given by
\mathscr{P}_{T_{i}}
, with
T_{i}
having a suitable uncertainty parameter role.

We denote the time intervals of the free evolutions as
t_{j}
, which is preceded by the
j\textsuperscript{th}
measurement and followed by
(j+1)\textsuperscript{th}
measurement. In other words,
t_{j}
is the waiting time between
j\textsuperscript{th}
and
(j+1)\textsuperscript{th}
measurements. To avoid cluttering of notation we will denote the multitime probability distribution as
\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{t})=\prod_{i=0}^{k}\mathscr{P}_{T_{i}}(t_{i})
, where now we use the labels

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}:=(t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbf{T}=(T_{0},T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})

(15)

for waiting times and the fuzziness parameters for each time interval, respectively. We can now define a finite temporal resolution process as

\displaystyle\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{k}\cdots\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{0}\,\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{t})\,\Upsilon,

(16)

and we will denote the average waiting time between each pair of measurements by

\tau_{i}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{i}\ t_{i}\ \mathscr{P}_{T_{i}}(t_{i}),

(17)

and we can visualize these concepts pictorially as in Fig.
1
.

We are interested in quantifying how different this out-of-equilibrium process, where time intervals are fuzzy, looks from an equilibrium process. To define the equilibrium process we follow the lead of earlier results, i.e. the initial state relaxes to the equilibrium state

\varpi:=\varpi_{0}=\lim_{T_{0}\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{0}(\varrho)\,\mathscr{P}_{T_{0}}(t_{0})\,dt_{0}=\mathcal{D}(\varrho)=\omega\otimes\gamma,

(18)

until an operation
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is performed, and subsequently the system relaxes again to an equilibrium state
\varpi_{1}=\mathcal{D}\mathcal{A}_{0}(\varpi)
until an operation
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is made, and so on for
k
-time-steps. Then we can define

\displaystyle\varpi_{i}

\displaystyle:=\lim_{T_{i}\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i-1}(\varpi_{i-1})\,\mathscr{P}_{T_{i}}(t_{i})\,dt_{i}=\mathcal{D}\mathcal{A}_{i-1}\cdots\mathcal{A}_{0}\mathcal{D}(\varrho),

(19)

for any
i=0,\cdots,k
as the equilibrium states after each intervention up to
\mathcal{A}_{i-1}
.

\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathbf{T}}
\Lambda
\rho
\mathsf{E}
\mathsf{S}
\mathcal{G}_{T_{0}}
\mathcal{A}_{0}
\mathsf{\Gamma}
\mathcal{G}_{T_{1}}
\mathcal{A}_{1}
\mathcal{G}_{T_{2}}
\mathcal{A}_{k-1}
\mathcal{G}_{T_{k}}
\cdots
\mathcal{A}_{k}
\Omega
\Lambda
\rho
\mathsf{E}
\mathsf{S}
\mathcal{D}
\mathcal{A}_{0}
\mathsf{\Gamma}
\mathcal{D}
\mathcal{A}_{1}
\mathcal{D}
\mathcal{A}_{k-1}
\mathcal{D}
\mathcal{A}_{k}
\cdots
\to
(\mathbf{T}\to\infty)
\omega

Figure 2:
Equilibration of quantum processes by finite temporal resolution:
This refers to a
k
-step process
\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathbf{T}}
time-averaged over each Hamiltonian evolution
(\mathcal{G})
within time-windows of width
\mathbf{T}=(T_{0},T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
remaining close to an equilibrium process
\Omega
. We define an equilibrium process as one dephased
(\mathcal{D})
with respect to the corresponding Hamiltonian at each time step. Equilibration is determined according to a set of operations
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}
on a subsystem
\mathsf{S}
, which can be correlated in time through an ancillary space
\mathsf{\Gamma}
, and is represented by a single tensor
\Lambda
. By definition, equality is attained in the limit of all
\mathbf{T}\to\infty
.

This is a sensible definition for the intermediate equilibrium states, which, however, is dependent on each operation
\mathcal{A}_{j}
. We can, however, define the equilibrium quantum process independently of the operations as

\displaystyle\Omega:=\lim_{\mathbf{T}\to\infty}\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}},

(20)

which is depicted in Fig.
2
as a set of dephasing maps
\mathcal{D}
at each timestep. This means then that we can write

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\tr[\Lambda\Omega]=\tr[\mathcal{A}_{k}\varpi_{k}]=\tr[\mathcal{A}_{k}\mathcal{D}\mathcal{A}_{k-1}\mathcal{D}\cdots\mathcal{A}_{0}\mathcal{D}(\varrho)],

(21)

for the expectation of a sequence of operations
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}
on the equilibrium process
\Omega
.

Since we can also express each finite averaging in the energy eigenbasis using the partial dephasing maps
\mathcal{G}_{T_{i}}
, defined in Eq. (
7
), we can similarly write
\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}}}=\tr[\mathcal{A}_{k}\varrho_{k}]
, where we now define

\varrho_{i}:=\mathcal{G}_{T_{i}}\mathcal{A}_{i-1}\cdots\mathcal{A}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{T_{0}}(\varrho),\quad\text{for}\quad i=0,1,\cdots,k,

(22)

as intermediate finite-time-averaged states after each intervention up to
\mathcal{A}_{i-1}
. As by definition
\lim_{T_{i}\to\infty}\mathcal{G}_{T_{i}}=\mathcal{D}
, the infinite-time limits
\mathbf{T}\to\infty
make
\Upsilon
indistinguishable from
\Omega
. We also depict this in Fig.
2
.

We may now generalize the left hand side of Eq. (
11
) with

|\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathbf{T}}-\Omega}|
, asking how different the statistics of a set of operations
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}
can be on a fuzzy clock process,
\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathbf{T}}
, as opposed to those in the equilibrium one
\Omega
. For clarity, let us present the case
H_{i}=H=\sum E_{n}P_{n}
, i.e. with a fixed Hamiltonian for all time-steps, and with
T_{i}=T_{j}=T
, i.e. with a clock with the same amount of fuzziness at all steps, as in Ref.
[
1
]
; we will then show how the general case reduces to this particular one.

2.1
Multitime equilibration due to finite temporal resolution
[
1
]
.

Given an environment-system-ancilla
\mathsf{(SE\Gamma)}
composite with initial state
\varrho=\rho\otimes\gamma
and initial equilibrium state
\varpi=\omega\otimes\gamma
, for any
k
-step process
\Upsilon
with an evolution generated by a time-independent Hamiltonian on
SE
at each step, and for any fuzzy multitime observable
\Lambda
corresponding to a sequence of temporally local operations
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k}
, each with fuzziness
T
acting on the joint
S
\mathbf{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
system,

\displaystyle\left|\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}}-\Omega}\right|\leq\mathds{A}_{k}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\|\mathcal{A}_{k:\ell+1}\|\left(\mathds{B}_{\ell}+\mathds{C}_{\ell}\right)\quad\text{with}\quad\mathds{A}_{k}:=\mathscr{S}_{T}^{k+1}\|\mathcal{A}_{k:0}\|\,\|\varrho-\varpi\|_{2},

(23)

where here
\mathscr{S}_{T}:=\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{nm}^{(T)}|
and
\mathcal{A}_{j:i}:=\mathcal{A}_{j}\cdots\mathcal{A}_{i}
is a composition of operations; the norm
\|\cdot\|
here stands for the norm on superoperators induced by the 2-norm,
\|\mathcal{X}\|=\sup_{\|\sigma\|_{2}=1}\|\mathcal{X}(\sigma)\|_{2}
; the first term is a single-time equilibration contribution, whereas the second term contains
k
multitime contributions where

\mathds{B}_{\ell}:=\|[\mathcal{G}_{T}^{k-\ell}-\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{\ell}]\varrho_{\ell}\|_{2},\qquad\mathds{C}_{\ell}:=\|[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{\ell}](\varrho_{\ell}-\varpi_{\ell})\|_{2},

(24)

with
\varrho_{i}
and
\varpi_{i}
intermediate finite-time averaged and equilibrium states at step
i
defined in Eq. (
22
) and Eq. (
19
), and where
[\cdot,\cdot]
denotes a commutator of superoperators.

Proof.

The main idea to bound the difference
\left|\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}}-\Omega}\right|
is to rearrange terms by commutation to obtain a single-time contribution with added correction terms. Let us slightly simplify notation by writing
\mathcal{G}_{i}=\mathcal{G}_{T_{i}}
and only labelling
\mathcal{D}
whenever it is relevant to know at which step this map is acting on. Let us first get this idea through with the
k=1
case,

\displaystyle\left|\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}}-\Omega}\right|=\left|\tr\left[\left(\bigcircop_{j=0}^{1}\mathcal{A}_{j}\mathcal{G}_{j}-\bigcircop_{j=0}^{1}\mathcal{A}_{j}\mathcal{D}_{j}\right)(\varrho)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\tr\left[\mathcal{A}_{1}\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\mathcal{G}_{1}\mathcal{G}_{0}-\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}\right)(\varrho)\right]+\tr\Big[\mathcal{A}_{1}\Big([\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{0}]\mathcal{G}_{0}-[\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{0}]\mathcal{D}\Big)(\varrho)\Big]\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\tr\left[\mathcal{A}_{1:0}\left(\mathcal{G}_{1:0}-\mathcal{D}\right)(\varrho)\right]\right|+\left|\tr\left\{\mathcal{A}_{1}[\mathcal{G}_{1}-\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{0}]\mathcal{G}_{0}(\varrho)\right\}\right|+\left|\tr\left\{\mathcal{A}_{1}[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{0}](\mathcal{G}_{0}-\mathcal{D})(\varrho)\right\}\right|,

(25)

where the third line follows by the triangle inequality (
|a-c|\leq|a-b|+|b-c|
, here with
b=\tr\{\mathcal{A}_{1}[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{0}]\mathcal{G}_{0}(\varrho)\}
). We now adopt the notation
\mathcal{A}_{j:i}:=\mathcal{A}_{i}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{A}_{j}
and from now on ommit the
\circ
symbol to denote composition of contiguous superoperators.

Then similarly we can do this for an arbitrary number of time-steps
k
,

\displaystyle\left|\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}}-\Omega}\right|=\left|\tr\left[\left(\bigcircop_{j=0}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}\mathcal{G}_{j}-\bigcircop_{j=0}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}\mathcal{D}_{j}\right)(\varrho)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\tr\left[\mathcal{A}_{k:0}\left(\mathcal{G}_{k:0}-\mathcal{D}\right)(\varrho)\right]+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\tr\Big[\mathcal{A}_{k:\ell+1}\Big([\mathcal{G}_{k:\ell+1},\mathcal{A}_{\ell}]\mathcal{G}_{\ell}\bigcircop_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{A}_{j}\mathcal{G}_{j}-[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{\ell}]\mathcal{D}_{\ell}\bigcircop_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{A}_{j}\mathcal{D}_{j}\Big)(\varrho)\Big]\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\tr\left[\mathcal{A}_{k:0}\left(\mathcal{G}_{k:0}\!-\mathcal{D}\right)(\varrho)\right]\right|+\!\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\left|\tr\left[\mathcal{A}_{k:\ell+1}[\mathcal{G}_{k:\ell+1}\!-\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{\ell}](\varrho_{\ell})\right]\right|+\!\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\left|\tr\left[\mathcal{A}_{k:\ell+1}[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{\ell}](\varrho_{\ell}-\varpi_{\ell})\right]\right|,

(26)

where
\varrho_{\ell}:=\mathcal{G}_{\ell}\bigcircop_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{A}_{j}\mathcal{G}_{j}(\varrho)
. Using
|\tr\mathcal{X}(\varrho)|\leq\|\mathcal{X}\|\|\varrho\|_{2}
, where for simplicity, here
\|\cdot\|
stands for the induced 2-norm defined as
\|\mathcal{X}\|:=\sup_{\|\sigma\|_{2}=1}\|\mathcal{X}(\sigma)\|_{2}
, which is a possible generalization of the operator norm for superoperators.
1
1

1

We can write the action of any bounded linear map
\mathcal{X}
(not necessarily
CP
) as
\mathcal{X}(\cdot)=\sum_{\mu}{L}_{\mu}(\cdot)R_{\mu}^{\dagger}
as a generalization of the Kraus representation in Section
1

[
83
]
. The inequality can then be seen to follow with Hölder’s inequality and the hierarchy of Schatten-norms. We use 2-norm for convenience in calculation.
Then we further can bound

\displaystyle\left|\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}}-\Omega}\right|\leq\left\|\mathcal{A}_{k:0}\right\|\,\left\|\left(\mathcal{G}_{k:0}-\mathcal{D}\right)(\varrho)\right\|_{2}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\left\|\mathcal{A}_{k:\ell+1}\right\|\,\left\|[\mathcal{G}_{k:\ell+1}-\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{\ell}](\varrho_{\ell})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\left\|\mathcal{A}_{k:\ell+1}\right\|\,\left\|[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{\ell}](\varrho_{\ell}-\varpi_{\ell})\right\|_{2},

(27)

which contains the terms
\mathds{B}
and
\mathds{C}
of Eq. (
24
) in the second and third terms of the inequality, and where the first term generalizes Eq. (
10
) as

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\mathcal{G}_{k:0}-\mathcal{D}\right)(\varrho)\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\tr\left|\sum_{n\neq m}G_{n_{k}{m}_{k}}^{(k)}\cdots{G}_{n_{0}m_{0}}^{(0)}P_{n}(\varrho)\,P_{m}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\neq m\\
n^{\prime}\neq m^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=0}^{k}G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}G_{m_{j}^{\prime}{n}_{j}^{\prime}}^{(j)}\tr\left[P_{n}\varrho\,P_{m}P_{m^{\prime}}\varrho\,P_{n^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\neq m}\prod_{j=0}^{k}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|^{2}\tr\left[P_{n}\varrho P_{m}\varrho\right]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=0}^{k}\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|^{2}\left\{\sum_{n,m}\tr[P_{n}\varrho P_{m}\varrho]-\sum_{n}\tr[P_{n}\varrho P_{n}\varrho]\right\}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=0}^{k}\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|^{2}\tr(\varrho^{2}-\varpi^{2})

\displaystyle=\|\varrho-\varpi\|_{2}^{2}\prod_{j=0}^{k}\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|^{2}.

(28)

where similarly in the last line
\tr(\varrho^{2}-\varpi^{2})=\|\varrho-\varpi\|_{2}^{2}
, because
\tr(\varrho\,\varpi)=\tr(\varpi^{2})
.
∎

2.2
.

In general, by definition the term
\mathscr{S}_{T}
, which depends on the waiting time distribution
\mathscr{P}_{T}
, converges to zero in increasing
T
, with the rate of convergence depending on the specific distribution. In particular, as we exemplified for the single-time case, for the uniform distribution on all time-steps as standard equilibration in Section
3
, we average over a time-window of width
T
around each
\tau_{i}
for all time-steps, with
\mathscr{P}_{T}=T^{-1}
in the interval
[\tau_{i}-T/2,\tau_{i}+T/2]
, and
\mathscr{P}_{T}=0
outside it. This yields

\left|G_{mn}^{(T)}\right|=|\mathrm{sin}(T\mathcal{E}_{mn})/T\mathcal{E}_{mn}|,

(29)

where the term
\mathscr{S}_{T}
then picks the smallest non-zero energy gap in the Hamiltonian.

Similarly, if the fuzziness corresponds to that of a half-normal distribution with variance
T
, then overall
\mathscr{S}_{T}
decays exponentially with

\displaystyle\left|G_{mn}^{(T)}\right|\sim\exp(-T\mathcal{E}_{mn}^{2})\left|1-\mathrm{erf}(i\sqrt{T}\mathcal{E}_{mn})\right|,

(30)

where
\mathrm{erf}
is the error function and
E_{m}-E_{n}=2\mathcal{E}_{mn}
. For both cases, if
T
is small,
\mathscr{S}_{T}
will also be vanishingly small whenever the energy gap
\mathcal{E}_{nm}
that maximizes
|G_{nm}^{(T)}|
is large enough, i.e.
\mathcal{E}_{nm}\gg{T}
. This property holds in general, since distributions
\mathscr{P}_{T}
can be approximated as uniform for small
T
or because the gaps
\mathcal{E}_{nm}
can be seen as a rescaling factor on
T
in the definition of
G_{nm}^{(T)}
.

The term
\mathds{A}_{k}
in Eq. (
23
) neglects temporal correlations and the operations
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}
are all composed as a single operation
\mathcal{A}_{k:0}=\mathcal{A}_{k}\cdots\mathcal{A}_{0}
. This is essence can be interpreted as a single-time contribution to equilibration. The two-norm distance satisfies
\|\varrho-\varpi\|_{2}^{2}\leq{1-(d_{E}d_{S})^{-1}}
as the ancillary input
\gamma
can be taken to be pure. As discussed above, this term is suppressed through the
\mathscr{S}_{T}
contributions when
i)
the averaging window, or equivalently the fuzziness of the clock
T
is large enough and
ii)
for small
T
whenever the energy gap maximizing the time-averaging
\left|G_{nm}^{(T)}\right|
factor is large with respect to
T
.

Now, we can bound further the terms
\mathds{B}_{\ell}
and
\mathds{C}_{\ell}
in Theorem
2.1
, which contain genuine multitime contributions relating to how well the intermediate states at step
\ell
equilibrate. Continuing from Eq. (
27
), we have

\displaystyle\mathds{B}_{\ell}=\left\|[\mathcal{G}_{k:\ell+1}-\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{\ell}](\varrho_{\ell})\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\right\|\,\left\|(\mathcal{G}_{k:\ell+1}-\mathcal{D})(\varrho_{\ell})\right\|_{2}+\left\|(\mathcal{G}_{k:\ell+1}-\mathcal{D})\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\varrho_{\ell})\right\|_{2},

(31)

where we used the triangle inequality on the commutator. Now from this inequality we have, similarly, for the first term,

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\mathcal{G}_{k:\ell+1}-\mathcal{D}\right)(\varrho_{\ell})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=\ell+1}^{k}\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|^{2}\left\{\sum_{n,m}\tr[P_{n}\varrho_{\ell}P_{m}\varrho_{\ell}]-\sum_{n}\tr[P_{n}\varrho_{\ell}P_{n}\varrho_{\ell}]\right\}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=\ell+1}^{k}\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|^{2}\tr[\varrho_{\ell}^{2}-\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell})\varrho_{\ell}]

\displaystyle=\|\varrho_{\ell}-\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell})\|_{2}^{2}\prod_{j=\ell+1}^{k}\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|^{2},

(32)

as
\tr[(\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell}))^{2}]=\tr[\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell})\varrho_{\ell}]
, whilst for the second term, with
\varrho^{\prime}_{\ell}=\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\varrho_{\ell})
,

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\mathcal{G}_{k:\ell+1}-\mathcal{D}\right)(\varrho_{\ell}^{\prime})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=\ell+1}^{k}\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|^{2}\left\{\sum_{n,m}\tr[P_{n}(\varrho_{\ell}^{\prime})P_{m}(\varrho_{\ell}^{\prime})]-\sum_{n}\tr[P_{n}(\varrho_{\ell}^{\prime})P_{n}(\varrho_{\ell}^{\prime})]\right\}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=\ell+1}^{k}\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|^{2}\tr[\varrho_{\ell}^{\prime\,2}-\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell}^{\prime})\varrho_{\ell}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\varrho_{\ell})-\mathcal{D}\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\varrho_{\ell})\|_{2}^{2}\prod_{j=\ell+1}^{k}\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|^{2},

(33)

so putting these together,

\mathds{B}_{\ell}\leq\prod_{j=\ell+1}^{k}\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|\left\{\|\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\|\|\varrho_{\ell}-\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell})\|_{2}+\|\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\varrho_{\ell})-\mathcal{D}\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\varrho_{\ell})\|_{2}\right\}.

(34)

This means that in the particular case of same evolution
H_{i}=H_{j}=H
and same fuzziness,
T_{i}=T_{j}=T
, we have

\mathds{B}_{\ell}\lesssim\mathscr{S}_{T}^{k-\ell},

(35)

so that, crucially, this term is suppressed overall in the width of the time-window
T
.

Finally, for
\mathds{C}_{\ell}
, notice that we can further simplify the last term of Eq. (
37
) as

\displaystyle\mathds{C}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\|[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{\ell}](\varrho_{\ell}-\varpi_{\ell})\|_{2}\leq\|\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell+1})\|_{2}+\|\varpi_{\ell+1}\|_{2}+\|\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\|(\|\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell})\|_{2}+\|\varpi_{\ell}\|_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell+1})-\varpi_{\ell+1}\|_{2}+\|\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\|\|\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell})-\varpi_{\ell}\|_{2},

(36)

and each term is the purity of a dephased state, which will decay as the inverse effective dimension of that state. This follows as in general,
\tr\left[(\mathcal{D}(\sigma))^{2}\right]\leq{d}_{\text{eff}}^{-1}(\sigma)
for any state
\sigma
, with equality for either pure states or non-degenerate Hamiltonians. On the other hand, when the control operations from
0
to
\ell
succeed in driving
\varrho_{\ell}
so that the action of the commutator does not dephase it significantly, the purity of
\varrho_{\ell}
may be large and thus
\mathds{C}_{\ell}
may become trivial (i.e. it approaches 1).

More concretely, the operations
\mathcal{A}_{j}
interleaved within the intermediate states
\varrho_{\ell}
and
\varpi_{\ell}
will relate in the multitime correction terms in Eq. (
24
) to how greatly they disturb either the finite-time averaged
\varrho_{j-1}
or the equilibrated
\varpi_{j-1}
. This is most evident in the term
\mathds{C}_{\ell}
, which can be bounded as well as
\mathds{C}_{\ell}\leq\|[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{\ell}]\|\|\varrho_{\ell}-\varpi_{\ell}\|_{2}
. The norm of the commutator can be written in terms of both the capacity of the operations
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
to generate coherences between different energy eigenspaces from equilibrium and the degree to which the operations can turn such coherences into populations. Environments in physical systems are typically much larger than the subsystems that can be probed, and, keeping in mind that the operations
\mathcal{A}_{j}
act only on subsystem
S
and the ancilla
\mathsf{\Gamma}
, the ability to generate and detect energy coherences should be severely limited in many physically relevant cases.

A more general version of the bound in Theorem
2.1
can thus be given as follows. Let us denote
\mathfrak{S}_{b:a}:=\prod_{j=a}^{b}\max_{n\neq{m}}|G_{n_{j}m_{j}}^{(j)}|
, then,

\displaystyle\left|\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}}-\Omega}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{S}_{k:0}\,\|\mathcal{A}_{k:0}\|\,\|\varrho-\varpi\|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{S}_{k:\ell+1}\,\|\mathcal{A}_{k:\ell+1}\|\,\bigg\{\|\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\|\|\varrho_{\ell}-\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell})\|_{2}+\|\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\varrho_{\ell})-\mathcal{D}\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\varrho_{\ell})\|_{2}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\|\mathcal{A}_{k:\ell+1}\|\left\{\|\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell+1})-\varpi_{\ell+1}\|_{2}+\|\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\|\|\mathcal{D}(\varrho_{\ell})-\varpi_{\ell}\|_{2}\right\},

(37)

so if we now take the Hamiltonian at each time-step to be a fixed
H=\sum{E}_{n}P_{n}
and we fix the fuziness to be the same at each step
T_{i}=T
, we now have
\FS_{b:a}=\mathscr{S}_{T}^{b-a}
where
\mathscr{S}_{T}:=\max_{n\neq{m}}\left|G_{nm}^{(T)}\right|
as in Eq. (
11
).

To summarize, our result in Theorem
2.1
shows that either subsystems or global coarse properties of a closed time-independent Hamiltonian system will display equilibration for multiple sequential operations with a temporal uncertainty or fuzziness provided:

i.

Both the initial and intermediate states have a significant overlap with the energy eigenstates.

ii.

The temporal fuzziness is large enough relative to the average measurement time or, equivalently, the energy gaps in the Hamiltonian are large enough with respect to the temporal fuzziness.

iii.

The disturbance by the operations on intermediate states is small.

3
Genuine multitime equilibration

In the previous section we have stressed the multitime nature of the bound in Theorem
2.1
, however, how can we be sure that this is not simply an elaborated example of the results of Ref.
[
54
]
(described in Section
3
)? Why would it not be possible to write the joint expectation
|\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\Upsilon}|
as a single Heisenberg picture operator acting on the initial state, i.e. to simply group all of the time evolutions and measurements into a single Hermitian operator
\mathfrak{F}
representing the measurement procedure acting on the initial state? This indeed suggests that a single-time equilibration bound, like the one in Eq. (
27
) would suffice to study equilibration in general quantum processes.

Let us then give a simple example that demonstrates that Theorem
2.1
indeed captures genuine multitime phenomena.

3.1
.

For simplicity, consider only two
CPTNI
interventions acting on
S
\mathbf{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
of the form

\mathcal{A}(\cdot)=\sum_{\mu}{a}_{\mu}{A}_{\mu}(\cdot)A_{\mu}^{\dagger},\qquad\mathcal{B}(\cdot)=\sum_{\mu}{b}_{\mu}{B}_{\mu}(\cdot){B}_{\mu}^{\dagger},

(38)

then the joint expectation for these operations interleaved with evolutions over time intervals
\delta{t}_{0}
and
\delta{t}_{1}
is

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\Upsilon}

\displaystyle=\tr[\mathcal{B}\,\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathcal{A}\,\mathcal{U}_{0}(\varrho)]=\sum{a}_{\mu}b_{\nu}\tr[B_{\nu}U_{1}A_{\mu}\,\mathcal{U}_{0}(\varrho)A_{\mu}^{\dagger}U_{1}^{\dagger}B_{\nu}^{\dagger}],

(39)

where as above,
\mathcal{U}_{\ell}=U_{\ell}(\cdot)U_{\ell}^{\dagger}
, with
U_{\ell}:=\exp(-iH_{\ell}{t}_{\ell})
with
H_{\ell}
the Hamiltonian at time-step
\ell
and
\varrho=\rho\otimes\gamma
the full initial
\mathsf{SE\Gamma}
state. Let us fix the basis for the Hamiltonians so that
H_{\ell}=\sum{E}_{n_{\ell}}{P}_{n}
as above. We can now move terms around using cyclicity of trace to get

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\Upsilon}

\displaystyle=\sum{a}_{\mu}b_{\nu}\tr[A_{\mu}^{\dagger}U_{1}^{\dagger}B_{\nu}^{\dagger}{B}_{\nu}U_{1}A_{\mu}\mathcal{U}_{0}(\varrho)]=\tr[\mathcal{A}\,\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\star}(\mathsf{B})\,\mathcal{U}_{0}(\varrho)],

(40)

where we define where
\mathcal{U}_{\ell}^{\star}(\cdot):=U_{\ell}^{\dagger}(\cdot)U_{\ell}
with
\mathsf{B}:=\sum\,b_{\mu}{B}_{\mu}^{\dagger}{B}_{\mu}
. The argument we refer to is that we can write this as
\langle{\mathfrak{F}}\rangle_{\varrho}=\tr[\mathfrak{F}\varrho(\delta t_{0})]
, where
\varrho(\delta t_{0}):=\mathcal{U}_{0}(\varrho)
and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}:=\mathcal{A}\,\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\star}(\mathsf{B}),

(41)

and obtain that
|\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}^{\mathbf{T}}}-\Omega}|=|\langle{\mathfrak{F}}\rangle_{\overline{\varrho}^{\mathscr{P}^{T_{0}}}-\omega}|
.

This happens to be the case if the interval
\delta{t}_{1}
is fixed, allowing fuzziness only in the first evolution time. In such case indeed we can simply apply the single-time result in Eq. (
10
), given that
\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\Omega}=\langle{\mathfrak{F}}\rangle_{\varpi_{0}}
with
\varpi_{0}=\mathcal{D}(\varrho)
as defined in Eq. (
18
).

However, when the fuzziness of the clock is present for each intervention, i.e. in both evolution times, we have

\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{T}}}}=\tr[\mathcal{B}\,\mathcal{G}_{1}\mathcal{A}\,\mathcal{G}_{0}(\varrho)],

(42)

where
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}=\mathcal{G}_{T_{\ell}}
, as we use in the proof of Theorem
2.1
and defined in Eq. (
7
), is the time-evolution superoperator finite-time averaged with respect to the probability distributions
\mathscr{P}_{T_{\ell}}
with a characteristic temporal fuzziness
T_{\ell}
. As defined in Eq. (
21
), the joint expectation with respect to the equilibrium process is

\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\Omega}=\tr[\mathcal{B}\,\mathcal{D}\,\mathcal{A}\,\mathcal{D}(\varrho)].

(43)

Now due to the double time average, it is impossible to write the difference of both quantities in terms of an operator expectation value, since

\displaystyle\left|\langle\Lambda\rangle_{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\mathscr{P}_{T}}-\Omega}\right|

\displaystyle=\tr[\mathcal{B}\,\mathcal{G}_{1}\,\mathcal{A}\,\mathcal{G}_{0}(\varrho)-\mathcal{B}\,\mathcal{D}\,\mathcal{A}\,\mathcal{D}(\varrho)]=\tr[\mathcal{B}\,\mathcal{G}_{1}\,\mathcal{A}\,\overline{\varrho}^{\mathscr{P}^{T_{0}}}-\mathcal{B}\,\mathcal{D}\mathcal{A}\,\varpi_{0}]

\displaystyle\neq\left|\langle{\mathfrak{E}}\rangle_{\overline{\varrho}^{\mathscr{P}^{T_{0}}}-\varpi_{0}}\right|,

(44)

for any operator
\mathfrak{E}
. Exceptions occur when we take the
T_{1}\to\infty
limit (so that
\mathcal{G}_{1}\to\mathcal{D}
), or if there is no fuzziness for a fixed
\delta{t}_{1}
(so that
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}
are replaced by a fixed
\mathcal{U}_{\delta{t}_{1}}
) as argued above. This means that in general the result in Theorem
2.1
constitutes an equilibration result that cannot be reduced to a single-time one.

4
Conclusions

Let us finally note that our approach to describe the operations that can act on the process is general in the sense that these are
CP
maps which can be correlated between time-steps and propagate information from their interactions with the subsystem
S
through the ancillary space
\mathsf{\Gamma}
. While these set a scale in all terms of the right-hand side of Eq. (
11
), they can also contribute to loosening it, potentially allowing to distinguish the fuzzy process from the equilibrium one within a finite time. It is not entirely clear, however, if a departure from equilibration is more readily accessible with a larger ancillary space
\mathsf{\Gamma}
, and, for long time fuzziness
T
, the upper-bound in Eq. (
11
) should remain close to zero.

Similar to the single-time standard case described in Section
3
, equilibration over multiple observations in open systems is expected intuitively through decoherence arguments
[
184
]
. The interplay with memory effects, through both the
E
and
\mathsf{\Gamma}
in the interventions is as yet not entirely clear, e.g., under which circumstances finite temporal resolution equilibration can occur without the dynamics being Markovian, i.e., memoryless, or if the temporal correlations among interventions through the ancillary space can display a departure from equilibration within a finite-time.

In the following Chapters we will see more clearly how the questions on the foundations of statistical mechanics can be posed in direct analogy in the context of quantum processes with respect to Markovianity. Whilst here we approached the question of equilibration in quantum processes somewhat pragmatically, we can conjecture a bridge between the characterization of the equilibration process and Markovianity akin to that between the generic, time-average equilibrium state and the Gibbs state for thermal equilibrium. The relationship between the two properties is as yet, however, not so transparent but we can certainly expect some progress in this direction in the near future.

Chapter 4
Markovian Typicality

Il n’y a de nouveau que ce qui est oublié.
1
1

1

There is nothing new except what has been forgotten
. The quote is sometimes attributed to Marie Antoinette, of whom Bertin was the dressmaker.

– Rose Bertin

As we saw in Chapter
1
, the quest towards understanding how thermodynamics emerges purely from quantum mechanical laws has seen a great deal of progress in recent years. Most prominently, equilibration on average deals with the dynamical explanation of how reversible and recurrent Schrödinger dynamics lead to irreversible reduced dynamics which converge and revolve around an equilibrium state, specifically telling us that time-dependent quantum properties evolve towards a certain fixed equilibrium value and stay close to it for most times. Moreover, we have seen that this dynamical convergence towards and around equilibrium holds more generally for subparts of closed systems under general quantum stochastic processes.

Whenever equilibration on average holds, it implies that the dynamics erases the information contained in the initial state of the respective system, however, there may still be non-Markovian memory of the initial state encoded in the temporal correlations between observables. Moreover, as we highlighted in Chapter
2
, all open quantum evolutions generated by a time-independent
SE
Hamiltonian are non-Markovian, i.e. we know that, as far as nature is concerned, non-Markovianity is the rule and Markovianity is the exception which at best is an idealization.

How can we explain then this apparent contradiction? Furthermore, the Born-Markov approximation has proven to be extremely fertile over the years, being applicable to a wide class of physical models and situations
[
100
,
101
,
8
,
102
,
103
,
148
,
149
]
.

There is now an evident parallel flow of ideas that we can draw from the emergence of statistical mechanics discussed in Chapter
1
; quantum systems dynamically equilibrate despite non-equilibrium being generic, in turn fulfilling the second law of thermodynamics, whose emergence can be explained from first principles such as entanglement, rather than from the equal a-priori probabilities postulate. Now we know that quantum processes satisfy an analogous form of dynamical equilibration and we can similarly ask if the emergence of forgetful processes can arise fundamentally rather than from ad-hoc assumptions or approximations such as the Born-Markov condition.

In Ref.
[
2
]
without resorting to the Born-Markov assumption or any other approximation, we formally proved that quantum processes are close to Markovian ones, when the subsystem
S
is sufficiently small compared to the whole
SE
, with a probability that tends to unity exponentially in the size of the latter. That is, we showed that Markovian processes are typical when these occur in small subsystems, with generic processes obeying a concentration of measure around Markovian ones. We also showed that, for a fixed global system size, it may not be possible to neglect non-Markovian effects when the process is allowed to continue for long enough, although detecting non-Markovianity for such processes would usually require non-trivial entangling resources. These results give birth to
almost
Markovian processes from closed dynamics analogous to the way in which entanglement supersedes the fundamental postulate of statistical mechanics.

1
Random quantum processes

The main approach we take to formally prove that Markovian processes are typical is generally speaking the one described in Section
3
; here as well we want to explore the statistical properties of quantum processes and study how in this case Markovian processes turn out to be exceptional. As we will see, however, we will need some additional mathematical concepts when it comes to the moments of the unitary group.

Similar to the case of quantum states, to approach the question of what sampling a random quantum process means, we require a probability measure that assigns non-vanishing probabilities to mathematically generic unitary dynamics on the closed
SE
composite. We similarly we achieved this by sampling the evolution from the unitarily invariant Haar measure, introduced in Section
1
. As we saw, this has the additional advantage of allowing employ random matrix theory techniques
[
185
,
186
,
187
,
188
,
189
,
190
,
191
]
and leads to the relatively straightforward application of concentration of measure results
[
70
,
192
,
193
]
.

Consider then a
k
-step quantum process
\Upsilon
on a
SE
composite of dimension
d_{\mathsf{SE}}=d_{\mathsf{S}}d_{\mathsf{E}}
, with initial state
\rho
and with unitary evolution given by unitary maps
\mathcal{U}_{i}=U_{i}(\cdot)\,U_{i}^{\dagger}
acting on the full
SE
at the
i
th
timestep. As we now are dealing with unitary evolution at several time-steps, we use the Haar measure to sample two distinct types of unitary
SE
evolution.

1.1
.

We refer to these two ways of sampling as:

i.

Random interaction: All
U_{i}
independently chosen.

ii.

Constant interaction:
U_{i}=U_{j},\quad\forall\leq{i}\neq{j}\leq{k}
.

We now depict this in Fig.
1
\mathsf{(a)}
. The entire set of unitaries enters into the process tensor as in the definition of the Choi state in Eq. (
52
). In the first case, the global system will quickly explore its entire (pure) state space for any initial state. The second case corresponds more closely to what one might expect for a truly closed system, where the Hamiltonian remains the same throughout the process. These correspond to two extremes; more generally, the dynamics from step to step may be related but not identical.

\mathsf{E}
\mathsf{S}
\Upsilon\sim\mu_{\mathsf{h}}
\rho\scriptstyle{\sim}\displaystyle\mu_{\mathsf{h}}
\mathcal{U}_{1}\scriptstyle{\sim}\displaystyle\mu_{\mathsf{h}}
\mathcal{U}_{2}\scriptstyle{\sim}\displaystyle\mu_{\mathsf{h}}
\cdots
\mathcal{U}_{k}\scriptstyle{\sim}\displaystyle\mu_{\mathsf{h}}
\mathsf{(a)}
\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}}
\mathsf{E}
\mathsf{S}
\varepsilon_{1}
\rho_{\mathsf{S}}^{(0)}
\mathcal{U}_{1}
\mathcal{U}_{2}
\varepsilon_{2}
\cdots
\mathcal{U}_{k}
\varepsilon_{k}
\mathsf{(b)}

Figure 1:
Sampling a quantum process at random and quantum Markovian processes:

\mathsf{(a)}
By the sampling of a random quantum process we mean a
k
-step process with a unitary evolution sampled from the Haar measure
\mu_{\mathsf{h}}
either according to a random interaction (independent
\mathcal{U}_{i}\neq\mathcal{U}_{j}
) or a constant interaction (all
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}_{j}
) as per Definition
1.1
.
(\mathsf{b})
We consider how distinguishable is a generic process sampled at uniformly at random from a Markovian one, which is mathematically equivalent to one where
SE
is initially uncorrelated,
\rho=\rho_{\mathsf{S}}^{(0)}\otimes\varepsilon_{1}
, with
E
being subsequently discarded and replaced afresh at every time-step.

Two main features of this approach now stand out. The first is that we are directly sampling unitary dynamics from the Haar measure which are not generally given as a time-independent Hamiltonian dynamics; this is slightly different from sampling a pure quantum state at random, as done in Section
3
, as in this case the type of dynamics generated by the Haar measure will be relevant. The second is precisely that the interaction or information flow between all parts of the whole
SE
composite will be relevant, i.e. no parts of the environment dimension are superfluous and in this sense we can think of having a strong interaction among all of the
SE
composite degrees of freedom.

2
The moments of the unitary group & average processes

As a
k
-step process tensor generically involves
k
unitary maps
\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{k}
. The fiducial state
\rho
can be taken to have undergone some evolution
\mathcal{U}_{0}
, which will let us interpret it as a random state. Back in Section
1
, when we distributed
SE
states according to the Haar measure, we only required the first and second moments of the unitary group to obtain a concentration of measure result. In this case, however, we are generically considering quantities involving
k+1
unitary maps, either all the same or all different and independently sampled.

We showed in Eq. (
48
) that the average quantum state drawn from the Haar measure is the maximally mixed state. For a random process, with all
\mathcal{U}_{i}\neq\mathcal{U}_{j}
independently chosen, we can simply apply the average independently over each unitary. First let us rewrite the definition of the Choi state representation of the process tensor,

\displaystyle\Upsilon=\tr_{\mathsf{E}}\left[\,\mathcal{U}_{k}\,\mathcal{S}_{k}\,\mathcal{U}_{k-1}\mathcal{S}_{k-1}\cdots\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\rho\otimes\tilde{\Psi}^{\otimes\,k}\right)\,\right]

\displaystyle=\!\!\sum_{\alpha,\ldots,\delta}\!\!\tr_{\mathsf{E}}\left[U_{k}\FS_{\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}}\!\cdots{U}_{1}\FS_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\rho\FS_{\delta_{1}\gamma_{1}}U_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots\FS_{\delta_{k}\gamma_{k}}U_{k}^{\dagger}\right]\otimes|\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}\!\cdots\beta_{k}\alpha_{k}\rangle\!\langle\delta_{1}\gamma_{1}\!\cdots\delta_{k}\gamma_{k}|,

(1)

where the sum runs over all Greek indices from
1
to
d_{\mathsf{S}}
, with
\tilde{\Psi}
being an unnormalized maximally entangled state acting in the respective
d_{\mathsf{S}}
-dimensional ancillary spaces
\mathsf{A}_{i}\mathsf{B}_{i}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{i}
are swaps between
S
and ancillary system
\mathsf{A}_{i}
at time-step
i
, and where
\FS_{\alpha\beta}=\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{E}}\otimes|\alpha\rangle\!\langle\beta|
. Full detail can be revisited around the definition in Eq. (
52
). Then let us denote as

\mathds{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}[f(U_{0},U_{1},\ldots,U_{k})]=\int_{\mathds{U}(d)}f(U_{0},U_{1},\ldots,U_{k})\,d\mu_{\mathsf{h}}(U_{0})\,d\mu_{\mathsf{h}}(U_{1})\cdots{d}\mu_{\mathsf{h}}(U_{k}),

(2)

the integration, or averaging, over the Haar measure independently over all time-steps, implicitly being over the different unitaries of the argument, so that we obtain

\displaystyle\mathds{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}[\Upsilon]=\sum_{\alpha,\ldots,\gamma}\tr_{\mathsf{E}}\left\{\mathds{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}\left[U_{k}\FS_{\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}}\cdots{U}_{1}\FS_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}U_{0}\rho\,U_{0}^{\dagger}\FS_{\delta_{1}\gamma_{1}}U_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots\FS_{\delta_{k}\gamma_{k}}U_{k}^{\dagger}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\otimes|\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}\alpha_{k}\rangle\!\langle\delta_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots\delta_{k}\gamma_{k}|

\displaystyle=\!\!\sum_{\alpha,\ldots,\gamma,\epsilon}\frac{\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{S}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}}\langle\epsilon\beta_{k}|\mathds{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}\left[U_{k-1}\FS_{\alpha_{k-1}\beta_{k-1}}\cdots{U}_{1}\FS_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}U_{0}\rho\,U_{0}^{\dagger}\FS_{\delta_{1}\gamma_{1}}U_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots\FS_{\delta_{k-1}\gamma_{k-1}}U_{k-1}^{\dagger}\right]|\epsilon\delta_{k}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\otimes|\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}\alpha_{k}\rangle\!\langle\delta_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots\delta_{k}\alpha_{k}|

\displaystyle=\!\!\sum_{\alpha,\ldots,\gamma,\epsilon}\frac{\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{S}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2}}\langle\epsilon\beta_{k-1}|\mathds{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}\left[U_{k-2}\FS_{\alpha_{k-2}\beta_{k-2}}\!\!\cdots{U}_{1}\FS_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}U_{0}\rho\,U_{0}^{\dagger}\FS_{\delta_{1}\gamma_{1}}U_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots\FS_{\delta_{k-2}\gamma_{k-2}}U_{k-2}^{\dagger}\right]|\epsilon\delta_{k-1}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\otimes|\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}\cdots\beta_{k-1}\alpha_{k-1}\rangle\!\langle\delta_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots\delta_{k-1}\alpha_{k-1}|\otimes 1_{\mathsf{A}_{k}\mathsf{B}_{k}}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\vdots

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{SA}_{1}\mathsf{B}_{1}\cdots\mathsf{A}_{k}\mathsf{B}_{k}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{\,k+1}},

(3)

where we introduced
\{|\epsilon\rangle\}
as a basis for
E
to perform each trace. This is, up to normalization of the maximally entangled states, a maximally mixed state in the full system-ancillary space, implying that the average process tensor is
maximally noisy
.

2.1
.

As a simple example consider
k=2
. Then the action of the average process tensor, say
\mathcal{T}_{2:0}^{\,\mathfrak{h}}
, on a pair of
CPTNI
operations
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2:0}^{\,\mathfrak{h}}[\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}]

\displaystyle=\tr_{\mathsf{in}}\left\{\mathds{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}[\Upsilon_{2:0}]\left[(\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{A}_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathrm{T}})\tilde{\Psi}\otimes(\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{A}_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathrm{T}})\tilde{\Psi}\right]\right\}=\frac{\tr[\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathds{1})]\tr[\mathcal{A}_{1}(\mathds{1})]}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{3}}\,\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{S}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{S}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}},

(4)

with equality for
TP
maps, i.e. with
\tr[\mathcal{A}_{i}(\mathds{1})]=\tr(\mathds{1})=d_{\mathsf{S}}
.

This generalizes similarly for any
k
, implying that the Haar average process tensor is maximally noisy, or analogous to quantum channel terms, completely depolarizing. Notice as well that this average process is Markovian, as the Choi state has a tensor product structure.

This fact relies upon the independent sampling of each unitary; however, if we consider a constant interaction, with all
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}_{j}
, we require a single integral equivalent to the
(k+1)
-moment of the unitary group, as defined in Eq. (
44
). In Section
1
we were able to compute the first and second moments by means of the twirl map and the Schur-Weyl duality. However, for higher-order moments this is not practical anymore, as per by the Schur-Weyl duality we need to consider all permutations and then relate back the twirl map with the moments of the unitary group. We can, however, capture in an abstract form the behaviour of the moments of the unitary group by means of an object called the
Weingarten function
(which in a sense also relies on the Schur-Weyl duality); this will let us, in particular, understand the asymptotic behaviour in dimensions and time-steps for random processes, as will become clear below.

The Weingarten function can be defined in different ways; it is a fairly complicated function to evaluate explicitly
[
187
,
194
]
but in any case, for the
n
-moments of the unitary group, it only depends on a given permutation of
n
and it gives a rational number in the dimension of the unitary in question. Tables with particular cases are often cited in the literature, which are helpful for computing lower moments, see e.g. Ref.
[
187
,
69
]
. An alternative is to perform calculations numerically
[
189
,
195
]
). For our purposes, we keep the definition of the Weingaten function as follows.

2.2
Weingarten function
[
187
]
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{n}
be the symmetric group on
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, and let
n\leq{d}
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{G}_{n}
, then the (unitary) Weingarten function,
2
2

2

\mathsf{Wg}
is named after Donald Weingarten
[
185
]
, who first studied asymptotic properties of the
n
-moments of
\mathds{U}(d)
. An explicit expression in terms of characters of symmetric groups and Schur functions was first derived by Benoît Collins
[
186
]
(later expanded in Ref.
[
188
]
), who coined the term.

\mathsf{Wg}
, is defined by

\mathsf{Wg}(\sigma,d)=\mathds{E}_{\mathsf{h}}\left[\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}U_{ii}U_{i\sigma(i)}^{*}\right],

(5)

where
U\sim\mu_{\mathsf{h}}
is a Haar distributed
d\times{d}
unitary matrix.

More explicit expressions of
\mathsf{Wg}
, such as the one derived in Ref.
[
186
]
, are outside the scope of this thesis, however, it is important to point out that
\mathsf{Wg}
depends not only on the particular permutation
\sigma
but on its cycle structure, that is, for example, for
n=3
, the cyclic permutations
\sigma=(1,2,3)
and
\sigma^{\prime}=(1,3,2)
, meaning
\sigma:1\to 2\to 3\to 1
and
\sigma^{\prime}:1\to 3\to 2\to 1
, will evaluate to the same value on
\mathsf{Wg}
, i.e.
\mathsf{Wg}(\sigma,d)=\mathsf{Wg}(\sigma^{\prime},d)
. We denote this cycle type with brackets as follows: given a set of positive integers
\{m_{i}\}
such that
m_{1}+m_{2}+\ldots+m_{\ell}=n
, then
[m_{1},m_{2},\ldots,m_{\ell}]
means cyclic permutations replacing
m_{1}
,
m_{2}
and
m_{\ell}
elements. For the previous example then the cycle type of
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
is
[3]
, similarly for the permutations
(1,2)(3)
,
(1,3)(2)
and
(2,3)(1)
it will be
[2,1]
, whilst for
(1)(2)(3)
we denote it as
\boldsymbol{1}^{3}:=[1,1,1]
.

The similarity with the expression for the
n
-moments of
\mathds{U}(d)
in Eq. (
44
) is now quite evident, and it implies the following.

2.3
The
n
-moments of the unitary group
[
186
,
187
]
.

Let
U\in\mathds{U}(d)
with
U\sim\mu_{\mathsf{h}}
be a a
d\times{d}
Haar random distributed unitary and
n\leq{d}
, then

\mathds{E}_{\mathsf{h}}\left[\,\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}U_{i_{\ell}{j}_{\ell}}U_{i^{\prime}_{\ell}{j}^{\prime}_{\ell}}^{*}\right]=\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in\mathfrak{G}_{n}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}\delta_{i_{\ell}{i}^{\prime}_{\sigma(\ell)}}\delta_{j_{\ell}{j}^{\prime}_{\tau(\ell)}}\,\mathrm{Wg}(\tau\sigma^{-1},d),

(6)

where
U_{ij}
is the
ij
th
entry of
U
.

A property that follows from this result, together with the invariance of the Haar measure, is that
\mathds{E}_{\mathsf{h}}\left[U_{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots{U}_{i_{n}j_{n}}U_{i^{\prime}_{1}{j}^{\prime}_{1}}^{*}\cdots{U}_{i^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}{j}^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}}^{*}\right]=0
, i.e. there has to be the same amount of
U_{ij}^{*}
as there are
U_{ij}

[
187
]
. As we have already computed the first and second moments in Eq. (
48
) and in Eq. (
50
) (together with Eq. (
53
)), albeit indirectly through the twirl map, we can nevertheless compare with the values in the table of Ref.
[
187
]
for the corresponding Weingarten functions,

\mathsf{Wg}([1],d)=\frac{1}{d},\qquad\mathsf{Wg}([\boldsymbol{1}^{2}],d)=\frac{1}{d^{2}-1},\qquad\mathsf{Wg}([2],d)=-\frac{1}{d(d^{2}-1)}.

(7)

For the first moment, the Weingarten function corresponds to the only coefficient of the 1-twirl, whilst for the second moment the Weingarten functions appear as coefficients in the
\alpha
,
\beta
functions of the 2-twirl in Eq. (
53
), that is, in fact we can write the 1-twirl
\Xi^{(2)}
and the 2-twirl
\Xi^{(2)}
as

\displaystyle\Xi^{(1)}[(\cdot)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Wg}([1],d)\tr[(\cdot)],

(8)

\displaystyle\Xi^{(2)}[(\cdot)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Wg}([\boldsymbol{1}^{2}],d)Z+\mathsf{Wg}([2],d)\swap Z,\quad\text{where}\quad{Z}=\tr[(\cdot)]\mathds{1}+\tr[\swap(\cdot)]\swap,

(9)

and so similarly, the
n
-twirl can be written in terms of the corresponding
n
Weingarten functions.

As mentioned before, this is generally not an easy thing to do and is usually done only for moments of small
n
; however, the asymptotic behaviour is usually one of interest in random matrix theory (or more generally in any theory of non-commutative random variables) and in our case it will help us establish our results for Haar distributed quantum processes. Ultimately, the asymptotic behavior of the moments of the unitary group boils down to that of the
\mathsf{Wg}
function.

2.4
.

In Ref.
[
187
]
it is shown that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Wg}(\sigma\in\mathfrak{G}_{n},d)\sim\frac{1}{d^{\,2n-\#\sigma}},\,\,\text{as}\,\,d\to\infty,

(10)

as a refinement of a result in Ref.
[
188
]
, where
\#\sigma
is the number of cycles of the permutation
\sigma
counting also fixed points (assignments from an element to itself,
\sigma(x)=x
).

Let us then consider the average process in the constant interaction case,
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}_{j}=\mathcal{U},\,\forall{i,j\leq{k}}
. As it is clear that for us
d=d_{\mathsf{SE}}
, we will omit the dimension dependence in
\mathsf{Wg}
. As we originally derived
3
3

3

In Ref.
[
2
]
we normalized each maximally entangled state entering the Choi state of the process tensor; the reason for this will be made clear in the following sections.
in Ref.
[
2
]
and we reproduce in Appendix
8.A
, the average
k
-step process tensor in the constant interaction case can be written by means of a set
\{|s_{i}^{(\prime)}\rangle\}_{s_{i}=1}^{d_{\mathsf{S}}}
of
S
system bases for
i=0,1,\ldots,k
as

\mathds{E}_{\mathsf{h}}[\Upsilon]=\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in\mathfrak{G}_{k+1}}\!\!\rho_{{}_{\tau(0);0}}\mathsf{Wg}(\tau\sigma^{-1})\Delta_{k,\sigma,\tau}^{(d_{\mathsf{E}})}|s_{\sigma(k)}\rangle\!\langle{s}_{k}|\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}|s_{\sigma(j-1)}s^{\prime}_{\tau(j)}\rangle\!\langle{s}_{j-1}s^{\prime}_{j}|,

(11)

with implicit sum over all repeated basis (
s^{(\prime)}_{i}
) indices, where here
\mathfrak{G}_{k+1}
is the symmetric group on
\{0,1,\ldots,k\}
, and with the definitions

\displaystyle\rho_{{}_{\tau(0);0}}

\displaystyle=\langle{e}^{\prime}_{\tau(0)}s^{\prime}_{\tau(0)}|\rho|e^{\prime}_{0}s^{\prime}_{0}\rangle,

(12)

\displaystyle\Delta_{k,\sigma,\tau}^{(d_{\mathsf{E}})}

\displaystyle=\delta_{e_{\sigma(k)}e_{k}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\delta_{e_{\sigma(\ell-1)}e^{\prime}_{\tau(\ell)}}\delta_{e_{\ell-1}e^{\prime}_{\ell}},

(13)

where
\{|e_{i}\rangle\}_{e_{i}=1}^{d_{\mathsf{E}}}
,
\{|e_{i}^{\prime}\rangle\}_{e_{i}^{\prime}=1}^{d_{\mathsf{E}}}
, with
i=0,1,\ldots,k
, also implicitly summed over all elements
e_{i}
and
e_{i}^{\prime}
, is a set of
E
bases and the
\Delta
term is simply a monomial in
d_{\mathsf{E}}
, with degree determined by
\sigma
and
\tau
.

The case
k=0
recovers
\mathds{E}_{\mathsf{h}}[\Upsilon_{0:0}]=\mathds{1}_{S}/d_{\mathsf{S}}
as expected, as no process occurs; the
1/d_{\mathsf{S}}
factor arises from the
\mathsf{Wg}([1],d_{\mathsf{SE}})
function, with the unifying
d_{\mathsf{E}}
factor coming from
\Delta_{0,\sigma,\tau}^{d_{\mathsf{E}}}
. In Appendix
1
we also write the case
k=1
for a superchannel and its purity, which can be seen to be close to the maximally mixed one and coincide with it in the large
E
limit, i.e. it coincides with the random interaction case.

As we saw in the whole Chapter
1
, the small subsystem limit,
d_{\mathsf{E}}\gg{d}_{\mathsf{S}}
, is of particular interest. For the average process tensor, we saw that the random interaction case in Eq. (
3
) is independent of
d_{\mathsf{E}}
, however for the constant interaction case we will get terms in inverse powers of
d_{\mathsf{E}}
arising from the
\mathsf{Wg}
functions. In particular, when looking at the limit
d_{\mathsf{E}}\to\infty
of Eq. (
11
), the only term that does not vanish is the one with
\sigma,\tau=\boldsymbol{1}^{k+1}
, i.e. with both permutations being identities, as these generate the most numerator powers in
d_{\mathsf{E}}
in the
\Delta_{k,\sigma,\tau}^{(d_{\mathsf{E}})}
term in Eq.(
13
) when summed over all
e_{i}
’s. In other words, we get the contribution from
\Delta_{k,\sigma,\tau}^{(d_{\mathsf{E}})}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}=1\\
e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{k}=1\end{subarray}}^{d_{\mathsf{E}}}\left[\delta_{e_{k}e_{k}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\delta_{e_{\ell-1}e^{\prime}_{\ell}}\delta_{e_{\ell-1}e^{\prime}_{\ell}}\right]=d_{\mathsf{E}}^{k+1},

(14)

and all other terms will vanish because of the
d_{\mathsf{E}}
powers in the denominator generated by the
\mathsf{Wg}
functions will dominate those from
\Delta_{k,\sigma,\tau}^{(d_{\mathsf{E}})}
. Given the asymptotic limit of
\mathsf{Wg}
in Eq. (
10
) we can see that indeed the least powers in
d
produced by it are those when
\sigma\tau^{-1}=\mathbf{1}^{k+1}
because
\#\boldsymbol{1}^{n}=n
, i.e. identity produces the greatest number of cycles, being the number of all possible fixed points. Finally, as
\sum_{\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}\varsigma^{\prime}_{0}}\langle\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}\varsigma^{\prime}_{0}|\rho|\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}\varsigma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle=\tr\rho=1
, we get

\displaystyle\mathds{E}_{\mathsf{h}}[\Upsilon]

\displaystyle\sim d_{\mathsf{E}}^{k+1}\mathrm{Wg}(\boldsymbol{1}^{k+1})\,\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{SA}_{1}\mathsf{B}_{1}\ldots\mathsf{A}_{k}\mathsf{B}_{k}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{SA}_{1}\mathsf{B}_{1}\ldots\mathsf{A}_{k}\mathsf{B}_{k}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{k+1}},\quad\text{when}\quad d_{\mathsf{E}}\to\infty,

(15)

coinciding with the average over a random interaction process.

3
A bound on non-Markovianity & average noisiness

As we saw previously in Section
5
, the process tensor leads to a well-defined Markov criterion from which it is possible to construct a family of operationally meaningful measures of non-Markovianity, many of which can be stated simply as
distances
between a process tensor’s Choi state
\Upsilon
and the closest Markovian one
\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}}
. We saw as well that
\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}}
must take the form of a tensor product of quantum maps
\mathcal{Z}_{i:i-1}
connecting adjacent pairs of time steps,
\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{Z}_{i:i-1}\otimes\rho_{\mathsf{S}}^{(0)}
.

We are now interested in studying the non-Markovianity of a generic quantum process. In Ref.
[
2
]
, in analogy with the seminal studies on equilibration that we introduced in Chapter
1
, we chose the measure of non-Markovianity defined in terms of the trace distance
D
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{1}:=\min_{\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}}}D\left(\Upsilon,\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}}\right)\propto\min_{\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}}}\|\Upsilon-\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}}\|_{1}

(16)

where
\|X\|_{1}:=\tr\sqrt{XX^{\dagger}}
is the trace norm (or Schatten 1-norm as defined by Eq. (
19
)). In particular, this trace-distance measure is related to relative entropy, briefly discussed in Section
5
, through the so-called quantum Pinsker inequality,
\mathcal{R}(\Upsilon\|\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}})\geq 2\,D^{2}(\Upsilon,\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}})
. Now, given that
\mathcal{N}_{1}
is a trace-distance measure, we demand that it satisfies
0\leq\mathcal{N}_{1}\leq 1
. This can be done imposing a normalization factor or directly normalizing the Choi states. We chose the latter,
4
4

4

The reasons might be said to be somewhat historical; one reason that motivated this choice is that it is more intuitive to think of properly normalized maximally mixed states as maximally noisy processes, as opposed to having an identity with an incorrect normalization factor.
with the caveat that we employ it consistently only for the purposes of employing this measure of non-Markovianity.

3.1
.

From this point onward we normalize the Choi state of the process tensor to unity, which is equivalent to defining these via properly normalized maximally entangled states, i.e. from here on we redefine
\Upsilon
as

\Upsilon=\tr_{\mathsf{E}}[\,\mathcal{U}_{k}\mathcal{S}_{k}\,\mathcal{U}_{k-1}\mathcal{S}_{k-1}\cdots\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathcal{S}_{1}\mathcal{U}_{0}(\rho\otimes\Psi^{\otimes\,k})],\quad\text{where}\quad\mathsf{\Psi}=\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{S}}}\tilde{\Psi},

(17)

where
\mathsf{\Psi}
are now rightful maximally entangled states, so that now
\Upsilon
satisfies
\tr[\Upsilon]=1
. This implies that now we explicitly have

\mathcal{N}_{1}:=\frac{1}{2}\min_{\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}}}\|\Upsilon-\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}}\|_{1}.

(18)

Now, we should point out that the choice of this measure is historical as well, as the trace distance is rather a distinguishability measure for quantum states rather than for quantum maps, and a distinguishability measure on Choi states would be directly a measure on these states rather than an operationally meaningful one on the corresponding processes. As we will see throughout the next chapter, this is not an issue in itself as we can tightly bound the measure
\mathcal{N}_{1}
with an operationally relevant measure of non-Markovianity known as the diamond distance, and the result we obtained in Ref.
[
2
]
is only changed by a multiplicative constant in a minor way, ultimately not changing its consequences. In fact, we will see that we can very often relate families of non-Markovianity measures, such as those given by Schatten norms, by at most a multiplicative factor. However, the results in this chapter and the numerical calculations were originally obtained with
\mathcal{N}_{1}
. We will thus, for consistency and for simplicity’s sake, present the results as originally derived with this measure in this thesis.
5
5

5

See Eq. (
14
) for the main result in this Chapter in terms of diamond norm.

To begin with, given the difficulty in computing and minimizing the Markovian Choi state, we may upper bound the distance
\mathcal{N}_{1}
by a trace distance with respect to the maximally mixed state, which as we have seen would correspond to the noisiest Markovian process possible,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{1}\leq{D}\left(\Upsilon,\frac{\mathds{1}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\right),

(19)

where the identity acting on
S
together with the
k+1
ancillas is implied. We may further bound this by considering the following cases separately.

1. Case

d_{\mathsf{E}}<d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}
: We notice that
\mathrm{rank}(\Upsilon)\leq{d_{\mathsf{E}}}
.
6
6

6

One may see this by looking at the pure state
{|\Phi\rangle\!\langle\Phi|\equiv\mathcal{U}(\Theta\otimes\Psi^{\otimes{k}})\,\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}}
with Schmidt decomposition
{|\Phi\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}|e_{i}s_{i}\rangle}
where
n=\min\left(d_{\mathsf{E}},d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}\right)
.
Letting
\gamma
be the diagonal matrix of up to
d_{\mathsf{E}}
non-vanishing eigenvalues
\lambda_{\gamma_{i}}
of the Choi state, we may write

\displaystyle\|\Upsilon-\frac{\mathds{1}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{\mathsf{E}}}\left|\lambda_{\gamma_{i}}-\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\right|+\sum_{j=d_{\mathsf{E}}+1}^{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\left|-\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\right|

\displaystyle=\|\gamma-\frac{\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{E}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\|_{1}+1-\frac{d_{\mathsf{E}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}},

(20)

where
|\cdot|
denotes the standard absolute value, so using the inequality
{\|X\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\mathrm{dim}(X)}\|X\|_{2}}
for a square matrix
X
, where
\|X\|_{2}=\sqrt{\tr(XX^{\dagger})}
is the Schatten 2-norm,

\displaystyle\|\Upsilon-\frac{\mathds{1}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{d_{\mathsf{E}}}\|\gamma-\frac{\mathds{1}_{\mathsf{E}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\|_{2}+1-\frac{d_{\mathsf{E}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{d_{\mathsf{E}}\tr[\Upsilon^{2}]+\frac{d^{2}_{\mathsf{E}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{4k+2}}-\frac{2d_{\mathsf{E}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}}+1-\frac{d_{\mathsf{E}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}},

(21)

Furthermore, applying Jensen’s inequality for the square-root,
\mathds{E}[\sqrt{X}]\leq\sqrt{\mathds{E}[X]}
, for any
\mathds{E}_{\mathsf{h}}
or
\mathds{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\mathcal{N}_{1}\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{d_{\mathsf{E}}\mathds{E}[\tr(\Upsilon^{2})]+\frac{d_{\mathsf{E}}^{2}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{4k+2}}-\frac{2d_{\mathsf{E}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}}+1-\frac{d_{\mathsf{E}}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\right).

(22)

2. Case

d_{\mathsf{E}}\geq{d}_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}
: This case is a small subsystem limit for most
k
. Directly applying
{\|X\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\mathrm{dim}(X)}\|X\|_{2}}
as before,

\displaystyle\|\Upsilon-\frac{\mathds{1}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\|\Upsilon-\frac{\mathds{1}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\|_{2}=\sqrt{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}\tr[\Upsilon^{2}]-1},

(23)

Similarly, taking the average over evolution, by means of Jensen’s inequality,

\mathds{E}\left[\mathcal{N}_{1}\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}\mathds{E}\left[\tr(\Upsilon^{2})\right]-1}.

(24)

This means that we can construct a piecewise function

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{k}(d_{\mathsf{E}},d_{\mathsf{S}})\equiv\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\frac{\sqrt{d_{\mathsf{E}}\,\mathds{E}[\tr(\Upsilon^{2})]-x}+y}{2}}&\text{if}\quad d_{\mathsf{E}}<d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}\\[7.22743pt]
\displaystyle{\frac{\sqrt{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}\mathds{E}[\tr(\Upsilon^{2})]-1}}{2}}&\text{if}\quad d_{\mathsf{E}}\geq{d}_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}\end{cases}\quad,

(25)

with
x=d_{\mathsf{E}}d_{\mathsf{S}}^{-(2k+1)}\left(1+y\right)
and
y=1-d_{\mathsf{E}}d_{\mathsf{S}}^{-(2k+1)}
, which provides an upper bound on the average non-Markovianity, i.e.

\mathds{E}[\mathcal{N}_{1}]\leq\mathcal{B}_{k}.

(26)

So similar to Section
3
, we now need to compute the average purity of
\Upsilon
.

The purity is a quantifier of the mixedness, or uniformity of eigenvalues of a positive operator, and as we saw as well in Section
3
, when computed on reduced states of bipartite systems it can serve as a quantifier of entanglement. In Appendix
8.B
and in Appendix
8.C
, we reproduce the computation of the expected purity of the Choi state of a process tensor
\mathbb{E}[\Upsilon^{2}]
in the random and the constant interaction pictures, respectively, which we derived originally in Ref.
[
2
]
. This average purity can be directly translated as a quantifier for
noisiness
of the quantum process itself and can serve as well to measure the entanglement between system and environment. Bear in mind again that here the process tensors are normalized to unity. The average purities take the form

\displaystyle\mathds{E}_{\mathfrak{h}}[\tr\left(\Upsilon^{2}\right)]=\frac{d_{\mathsf{E}}^{2}-1}{d_{\mathsf{E}}(d_{\mathsf{SE}}+1)}\left(\frac{d_{\mathsf{E}}^{2}-1}{d_{\mathsf{SE}}^{2}-1}\right)^{k}+\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{E}}},

(27)

for the random interaction picture, where we have assumed that the fiducial state
\rho
is pure, and

\displaystyle\mathds{E}_{\mathsf{h}}[\tr\left(\Upsilon^{2}\right)]=d_{\mathsf{S}}^{-2k}\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in\mathfrak{G}_{2k+2}}\mathsf{Wg}(\tau\sigma^{-1})\,\rho_{{}_{\tau(0);k+1}}\rho_{{}_{\tau(k+1);0}}\,\Delta_{k,\sigma,\tau}^{(d_{\mathsf{E}},d_{\mathsf{S}})},

(28)

in the constant interaction case, where
\rho_{\tau(\cdot);\ell}
uses the same notation as in Eq. (
12
) and is shown explicitly in Eq. (
29
) on Appendix
8.C
, and
\Delta
is a product, scaling with
k
, of monomials in
d_{\mathsf{E}}
and
d_{\mathsf{S}}
depending on permutations
\sigma
and
\tau
, shown in full in Eq. (
30
) also within Appendix
8.C
.

1
Limiting cases

In both the constant and random interaction cases, the bound
\mathcal{B}_{k}
on the non-Markovianity
\mathcal{N}_{1}
is a well-behaved rational function of
d_{\mathsf{E}}
,
d_{\mathsf{S}}
and
k
. In the constant interaction case, Eq. (
28
) takes a non-trivial form mainly because of the
\mathsf{Wg}
function (which is intrinsic to the Haar-unitary averaging in the constant interaction picture). However, due to the results in Ref.
[
187
,
188
]
, we can still study analytically the behaviour of the bound
\mathcal{B}_{k}
for both cases in the following limits.

Small subsystem limit

For both interaction pictures,

\displaystyle\lim_{d_{\mathsf{E}}\to\infty}\mathds{E}[\tr(\Upsilon^{2})]=\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}},

(29)

which corresponds to the purity of the maximally mixed state. Note that the averaging occurs after computing the purity independently of which case is considered, i.e. this does not correspond to the purity of the average process but the average purity of a process.

This implies that on average, in the small subsystem limit, a process will be indistinguishable from the maximally noisy (and hence Markovian) one,

\displaystyle\lim_{d_{\mathsf{E}}\to\infty}\mathds{E}[\mathcal{N}_{1}]=\lim_{d_{\mathsf{E}}\to\infty}\mathcal{B}_{k}(d_{\mathsf{E}},d_{\mathsf{S}})=0,

(30)

and from Eq. (
27
) we know it does so at a rate
\mathcal{O}(1/d_{\mathsf{E}})
in the random interaction case.

Long time limit

The other interesting limiting case is the one where the
SE
dimension is fixed, but the number of time steps is taken to be very large. The resulting process encodes all high order correlation functions between observables over a long period of time. Again for both cases, the expected purity in this limit goes as

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathds{E}[\tr(\Upsilon^{2})]=\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{E}}},

(31)

which corresponds to the maximally mixed purity of the environment. This implies that the Choi state of the full
SE
unitary process is maximally entangled between
S
and
E
. In this limit, we get correspondingly

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathds{E}[\mathcal{N}_{1}]\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{B}_{k}(d_{\mathsf{E}},d_{\mathsf{S}})=1,

(32)

meaning only that we cannot say much about non-Markovianity in this limit, i.e. a typical process in this limit could be highly non-Markovian. Indeed, we expect this to be the case, since the finite-dimensional
SE
space will have a finite recurrence time.

Average state purity limit

As expected as well, our result generalizes the well-known result for quantum states, i.e., when
k=0
. In this case we recover the average purity

\mathds{E}[\tr(\rho_{S}^{2})]=\frac{d_{\mathsf{E}}+d_{\mathsf{S}}}{d_{\mathsf{SE}}+1},

(33)

where
\rho_{S}\equiv\Upsilon_{0:0}=\tr_{E}(U\rho\,U^{\dagger})
, as we re-derived in Eq. (
57
).

4
Concentration around Markovian processes

From the discussion in Section
1
it seems clear that we have almost all the ingredients to turn the statements above into a concentration of measure result. We proved the following.

4.1
Concentration of measure around Markovian processes
[
2
]
.

Let
\Upsilon\sim\mu_{\mathsf{h}}
be a
k
-step quantum process undergone by a
d_{\mathsf{S}}
-dimensional subsystem of a larger
d_{\mathsf{SE}}
-dimensional composite, sampled at random according to the Haar measure, then for any
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathds{P}_{\mathsf{h}}[\,\mathcal{N}_{1}\geq\mathcal{B}_{k}(d_{\mathsf{E}},d_{\mathsf{S}})+\delta]\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\mathscr{C}(d_{\mathsf{E}},d_{\mathsf{S}})\delta^{2}},

(34)

where
\mathscr{C}(d_{\mathsf{E}},d_{\mathsf{S}})=c\,d_{\mathsf{SE}}\left(\frac{d_{\mathsf{S}}-1}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{k+1}-1}\right)^{2}
with
c=1/4
for a constant interaction process and
c=(k+1)/4
for a random interaction process. The function
\mathcal{B}_{k}
is an upper bound on the expected non-Markovianity
\mathds{E}[\mathcal{N}_{1}]
, given in Eq. (
25
), whose details depend on the way in which processes are sampled.

Proof.

We have previously obtained the function
\mathcal{B}_{k}
completely, so we now derive the Lipschitz constants
\mathscr{L}
for both the constant and the random interaction cases, as well as the concentration rate.

Lipschitz constant (constant interaction)

Let
\tau:\mathds{U}(d_{\mathsf{SE}})\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
\tau(U)=D\left(\Upsilon(U),\frac{\mathds{1}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\|\Upsilon(U)-\frac{\mathds{1}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\|_{1}
, where we explicitly mean
\Upsilon(U)=\tr_{E}[\mathsf{U}_{k:0}\Theta\mathsf{U}_{k:0}^{\dagger}]
where
\mathsf{U}_{k:0}=U_{k}\mathcal{S}_{k}\cdots{U}_{1}\mathcal{S}_{1}U_{0}
and with
\Theta=\rho\otimes\mathsf{\Psi}^{\otimes{k}}
a pure state, then for all
V\in\mathds{U}(d_{\mathsf{SE}})
,

\displaystyle|\tau({U})-\tau({V})|

\displaystyle=\left|D\left(\Upsilon(U),\frac{\mathds{1}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\right)-D\left(\Upsilon(V),\frac{\mathds{1}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq{D}(\Upsilon(U),\Upsilon(V))

\displaystyle\leq{D}(\mathsf{U}_{k:0}\Theta\mathsf{U}_{k:0}^{\dagger},\mathsf{V}_{k:0}\Theta\mathsf{V}_{k:0}^{\dagger}),

(35)

where here similarly
\mathsf{V}_{k:0}=V_{k}\mathcal{S}_{k}\cdots{V}_{1}\mathcal{S}_{1}V_{0}
.

Consider now different labelings for the unitary at each time step so that we can easily track each one. By the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{U}_{k:0}\Theta\mathsf{U}_{k:0}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{V}_{k:0}\Theta\mathsf{V}_{k:0}^{\dagger}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathsf{U}_{k:0}\Theta\mathsf{U}_{k:0}^{\dagger}-U_{k}\mathcal{S}_{k}\mathsf{V}_{k-1:0}\Theta\mathsf{V}_{k-1:0}^{\dagger}\mathcal{S}_{k}^{\dagger}{U}_{k}^{\dagger}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\|U_{k}\mathcal{S}_{k}\mathsf{V}_{k-1:0}\Theta\mathsf{V}_{k-1:0}^{\dagger}\mathcal{S}_{k}^{\dagger}{U}_{k}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{V}_{k:0}\Theta\mathsf{V}_{k:0}^{\dagger}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|\mathsf{U}_{k-1:0}\Theta\mathsf{U}_{k-1:0}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{V}_{k-1:0}\Theta\mathsf{V}_{k-1:0}^{\dagger}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\|U_{k}\mathcal{S}_{k}\mathsf{V}_{k-1:0}\Theta\mathsf{V}_{k-1:0}^{\dagger}\mathcal{S}_{k}^{\dagger}{U}_{k}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{V}_{k:0}\Theta\mathsf{V}_{k:0}^{\dagger}\|_{1},

(36)

where it is clear by context that
U
stands for
U\otimes\mathds{1}
.

Now we use Lemma 1 of Ref.
[
196
]
, which states that

\displaystyle\|A\sigma{A}^{\dagger}-B\sigma{B}^{\dagger}\|_{1}\leq 2\|A-B\|_{2}

(37)

for two unitaries
A
,
B
and any
\sigma
. For the simplest case with
k=1
, this gives

\displaystyle\|U_{1}\mathcal{S}_{1}V_{0}\Theta{V}_{0}^{\dagger}\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\dagger}{U}_{1}^{\dagger}-V_{1}\mathcal{S}_{1}V_{0}\Theta{V}_{0}^{\dagger}\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\dagger}{V}_{1}^{\dagger}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\|\frac{\sum}{d_{\mathsf{S}}}(U_{1}\FS_{\alpha\beta}V_{0}\rho{V}_{0}^{\dagger}\FS_{\gamma\delta}U_{1}^{\dagger}-{V}_{1}\FS_{\alpha\beta}V_{0}\rho{V}_{0}^{\dagger}\FS_{\gamma\delta}V_{1}^{\dagger})\otimes|\beta\alpha\rangle\!\langle\gamma\delta|\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sum_{\alpha\beta}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}}\tr\bigg[\sum_{\gamma\delta}(U_{1}\FS_{\alpha\beta}V_{0}\rho{V}_{0}^{\dagger}\FS_{\gamma\delta}U_{1}^{\dagger}-{V}_{1}\FS_{\alpha\beta}V_{0}\rho{V}_{0}^{\dagger}\FS_{\gamma\delta}V_{1}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad(U_{1}\FS_{\beta\alpha}V_{0}\rho{V}_{0}^{\dagger}\FS_{\delta\gamma}U_{1}^{\dagger}-{V}_{1}\FS_{\beta\alpha}V_{0}\rho{V}_{0}^{\dagger}\FS_{\delta\gamma}V_{1}^{\dagger})\bigg]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sum_{\alpha\beta}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}}\|U_{1}\FS_{\alpha\beta}V_{0}\rho{V}_{0}^{\dagger}\left(\sum\FS_{\gamma\delta}\right)U_{1}^{\dagger}-{V}_{1}\FS_{\alpha\beta}V_{0}\rho{V}_{0}^{\dagger}\left(\sum\FS_{\gamma^{\prime}\delta^{\prime}}\right)V_{1}^{\dagger}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{d_{\mathsf{S}}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{d_{\mathsf{S}}}\|U_{1}-V_{1}\|_{2}=2d_{\mathsf{S}}\|U_{1}-V_{1}\|_{2},

(38)

and doing similarly, for the
i
-th step,

\displaystyle\|U_{i}\mathcal{S}_{i}\mathsf{V}_{i-1:0}\Theta\mathsf{V}_{i-1:0}^{\dagger}\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\dagger}{U}_{i}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{V}_{i:0}\Theta\mathsf{V}_{i:0}^{\dagger}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2d_{\mathsf{S}}^{i}\|U_{i}-V_{i}\|.

(39)

Thus bounding iteratively expression (
36
), it follows that

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{U}_{k:0}\Theta\mathsf{U}_{k:0}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{V}_{k:0}\Theta\mathsf{V}_{k:0}^{\dagger}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}d_{\mathsf{S}}^{\ell}\|U_{\ell}-V_{\ell}\|_{2},

(40)

finally giving

\displaystyle|\tau(U)-\tau(V)|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{k+1}-1}{d_{\mathsf{S}}-1}\right)\|U-V\|_{2}.

(41)

Lipschitz constant (random interaction)

On the other hand, for the ergodic case, let
\mathds{U}^{\times(k+1)}(d)=\underbrace{\mathds{U}(d)\times\cdots\times\mathds{U}(d)}_{k+1\,\text{times}}
be the
k+1
Cartesian product space of
d
-dimensional unitary groups, then we define
\zeta:\mathds{U}^{\times(k+1)}(d_{\mathsf{SE}})\to\mathbb{R}
by
\zeta(\vec{U})=D\left(\Upsilon(\vec{U}),\frac{\mathds{1}}{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}}\right)
where now
\vec{U}=(U_{0},\cdots,U_{k})
. Similarly as before, we now have

\displaystyle\left|\zeta(\vec{U})-\zeta(\vec{V})\right|\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}d_{\mathsf{S}}^{\ell}\|U_{\ell}-V_{\ell}\|_{2},

(42)

and we may let the metric on
\mathds{U}^{\times(k+1)}(d_{\mathsf{SE}})
be the 2-product metric
\delta_{\mathbb{U}}
defined
[
197
]
by

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathbb{U}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})=\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|x_{\ell}-y_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}},

(43)

which then satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}d_{\mathsf{S}}^{\ell}\|U_{\ell}-V_{\ell}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}d_{\mathsf{S}}^{\ell}\sqrt{\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{k}\|U_{\ell^{\prime}}-V_{\ell^{\prime}}\|_{2}^{2}}=\left(\frac{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{k+1}-1}{d_{\mathsf{S}}-1}\right)\delta_{\mathbb{U}}(\vec{U},\vec{V}),

(44)

and thus we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\zeta(\vec{U})-\zeta(\vec{V})\right|\leq\left(\frac{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{k+1}-1}{d_{\mathsf{S}}-1}\right)\delta_{\mathbb{U}}(\vec{U},\vec{V}),

(45)

so the (bound on) Lipschitz constants coincide with

\mathscr{L}\leq\frac{d_{\mathsf{S}}^{k+1}-1}{d_{\mathsf{S}}-1},

(46)

which essentially behaves as
\mathcal{O}\left(d_{\mathsf{S}}^{k}\right)
.

The concentration function (constant interaction)

We now make use of a result related to the Gromov-Bishop inequality (see e.g. Theorem 7 in Ref.
[
198
]
) stating that if
\mathrm{Ric}(M)\geq\mathrm{Ric}(\Sigma^{n}(R))=\frac{n-1}{R^{2}}
for an
n
-dimensional manifold
M
, where
\Sigma^{n}(R)
is the
n
-dimensional sphere of radius
R
and
\mathrm{Ric}(X)
is the infimum of diagonal elements of the Ricci curvature tensor on
X
, then the respective concentration functions satisfy
\alpha_{M}(x)\leq\alpha_{\Sigma^{n}(R)}(x)\leq\exp\left[-\frac{x^{2}(n-1)}{2R^{2}}\right]

[
70
]
.

For the constant interaction case, the corresponding manifold is the group manifold
\mathscr{U}
of
\mathds{U}(d)
(where here
d=d_{\mathsf{SE}}
), which is diffeomorphic to
\mathds{SU}(d)\times\Sigma^{1}(1)

[
70
]
, where
\mathds{SU}(d)
denotes the special unitary group (i.e. with added
\det(U)=1
for any element) and thus has
\mathrm{Ric}(\mathscr{U})=d/2
and
\dim(\mathscr{U})=d^{2}
. Then it follows that
\mathrm{Ric}(\mathscr{U})\geq\mathrm{Ric}(\Sigma^{d^{2}}(r))
if
r^{2}\geq 2(d^{2}-1)/d
and hence, taking the minimal case,

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{\mathscr{U}}}(\delta/\mathscr{L})\leq\alpha_{\Sigma^{d^{2}}(r)}(\delta/\mathscr{L})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}d_{\mathsf{SE}}}{4\mathscr{L}^{2}}\right).

(47)

The concentration function (random interaction)

For the random interaction case the corresponding manifold is the group manifold
\mathbf{U}
of the
k+1
Cartesian product space
\mathds{U}(d)\times\cdots\times\mathds{U}(d)
. In this case
\mathrm{Ric}(\mathbf{U})=(k+1)d/2
and
\dim(\mathbf{U})=(k+1)d^{2}
. Then it follows that
\mathrm{Ric}(\mathbf{U})\geq\mathrm{Ric}(\Sigma^{(k+1)d^{2}}(\mathcal{R}))
if
\mathcal{R}^{2}\geq 2[(k+1)d^{2}-1]/[d(k+1)]
and hence, taking the minimal case,

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{\mathbf{U}}}(\delta/\mathscr{L})\leq\alpha_{\Sigma^{(k+1)d_{\mathsf{SE}}^{2}}(\mathcal{R})}(\delta/\mathscr{L})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}(k+1)d_{\mathsf{SE}}}{4\mathscr{L}^{2}}\right).

(48)

The factor
d_{\mathsf{SE}}\mathscr{L}^{-2}
behaves as
\mathcal{O}\left(d_{\mathsf{E}}d_{\mathsf{S}}^{-2k+1}\right)
, so both concentration functions will be small whenever
d_{\mathsf{E}}\gg{d}_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k}
.
∎

Theorem
4.1
assures that the probability for the non-Markovianity
\mathcal{N}_{1}
to exceed a function of
k
,
d_{\mathsf{S}}
and
d_{\mathsf{E}}
, that becomes very small in the large
d_{\mathsf{E}}
limit, itself becomes small in that limit. Our result is meaningful when both
\mathcal{B}_{k}+\delta
and
\exp\left(-\mathscr{C}\delta^{2}\right)
are small; the latter is fulfilled in the
small subsystem
or
large environment
limit, which in our setting means
d_{\mathsf{E}}\gg{d}_{\mathsf{S}}^{2k+1}
. We may also state the minimal value for
\delta
that, assuming the large environment limit, renders both sides small,
7
7

7

Similar to Ref.
[

]
detailed in Section
3
, here we look for an
x>0
such that
\delta=d_{\mathsf{E}}^{-x}
and
\delta^{2}d_{\mathsf{E}}=d_{\mathsf{E}}^{x}
.
i.e., such that
\delta^{2}d_{\mathsf{E}}\gg 1\gg\delta
; this is fulfilled for
\delta=d_{\mathsf{E}}^{-1/3}
. A geometrical cartoon to illustrate the result is presented in Fig.

.

Theorem
4.1
can be said to state that
almost all quantum processes are almost Markovian
.

Figure 2:
Concentration around Markovian processes in large dimensional environments:

\mathsf{(a)}
A geometric cartoon of our main result in a space of process tensors: the probability of the non-Markovianity
\mathcal{N}_{1}
of deviating from
\mathcal{B}_{k}
by some
\delta>0

. In

\Upsilon
on large dimensional environments (such that

) concentrate around the Markovian ones
\Upsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathrm{M})}}
.

Our results imply that it is fundamentally hard to observe non-Markovianity in a typical process and thus go some way to explaining the overwhelming success of Markovian theories.

Specifically, Theorem
4.1
shows that even when interacting strongly with the wider composite system, a subsystem will typically undergo highly Markovian dynamics when the rest of the system has a sufficiently large dimension, and that the probability to be significantly non-Markovian vanishes with the latter. Our main result formalizes the notion that in the large environment limit a quantum process, taken uniformly at random, will be almost Markovian with very high probability. This corroborates the common understanding of the Born-Markov approximation, discusses in Section
3
, but, crucially, we make no assumptions about weak coupling between
E
and
S
. Instead, in the Haar random interactions we consider, every part of the system typically interacts significantly with every part of
E
. This is in contrast to many open systems models, even those with superficially infinite dimensional baths, where the effective dimension of the environment is relatively small
[
199
]
; it can always be bounded by a function of time scales in the system-environment Hamiltonian
[
200
]
, which could be encoded in a bath spectral density. Our result is also more general than the scenario usually considered, since it accounts for interventions and thus the flow of information between
S
and
E
across multiple times.

1
Numerical sampling

To support our results, we sampled process tensors
\Upsilon
numerically in the random interaction case and computed their corresponding average non-Markovianity
\mathds{E}[\mathcal{N}_{1}]
as a function of environment dimension
d_{\mathsf{E}}
for a fixed system dimension
d_{\mathsf{S}}=2
, obtaining the behaviour shown in Fig.
3
. The details on how this was done can be seen in Appendix
9
.

For constant interaction the numerical results are practically indistinguishable from those in the random case, but as mentioned, the analytical bound
\mathcal{B}_{k}
is much harder to compute exactly. This suggests that either a simpler bound exists or that it might be possible to simplify the one we have obtained.

) and Eq. (
